persistent markdown files as your \"working memory on disk.\"\n\n## Important: Where Files Go\n\nWhen using this skill:\n\n- **Templates** are stored in the skill directory at `${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}/templates/`\n- **Your planning files** (`task_plan.md`, `findings.md`, `progress.md`) should be created in **your project directory** — the folder where you're working\n\n| Location | What Goes There |\n|----------|-----------------|\n| Skill directory (`${CLAUDE_PLUGIN_ROOT}/`) | Templates, scripts, reference docs |\n| Your project directory | `task_plan.md`, `findings.md`, `progress.md` |\n\nThis ensures your planning files live alongside your code, not buried in the skill installation folder.\n\n## Quick Start\n\nBefore ANY complex task:\n\n1. **Create `task_plan.md`** in your project — Use [templates/task_plan.md](templates/task_plan.md) as reference\n2. **Create `findings.md`** in your project — Use [templates/findings.md](templates/findings.md) as reference\n3. **Create `progress.md`** in your project — Use [templates/progress.md](templates/progress.md) as reference\n4. **Re-read plan before decisions** — Refreshes goals in attention window\n5. **Update after each phase** — Mark complete, log errors\n\n> **Note:** All three planning files should be created in your current working directory (your project root), not in the skill's installation folder.\n\n## The Core Pattern\n\n```\nContext Window = RAM (volatile, limited)\nFilesystem = Disk (persistent, unlimited)\n\n→ Anything important gets written to disk.\n```\n\n## File Purposes\n\n| File | Purpose | When to Update |\n|------|---------|----------------|\n| `task_plan.md` | Phases, progress, decisions | After each phase |\n| `findings.md` | Research, discoveries | After ANY discovery |\n| `progress.md` | Session log, test results | Throughout session |\n\n## Critical Rules\n\n### 1. Create Plan First\nNever start a complex task without `task_plan.md`. Non-negotiable.\n\n### 2. The 2-Action Rule\n> \"After every 2 view/browser/search operations, IMMEDIATELY save key findings to text files.\"\n\nThis prevents visual/multimodal information from being lost.\n\n### 3. Read Before Decide\nBefore major decisions, read the plan file. This keeps goals in your attention window.\n\n### 4. Update After Act\nAfter completing any phase:\n- Mark phase status: `in_progress` → `complete`\n- Log any errors encountered\n- Note files created/modified\n\n### 5. Log ALL Errors\nEvery error goes in the plan file. This builds knowledge and prevents repetition.\n\n```markdown\n## Errors Encountered\n| Error | Attempt | Resolution |\n|-------|---------|------------|\n| FileNotFoundError | 1 | Created default config |\n| API timeout | 2 | Added retry logic |\n```\n\n### 6. Never Repeat Failures\n```\nif action_failed:\n    next_action != same_action\n```\nTrack what you tried. Mutate the approach.\n\n## The 3-Strike Error Protocol\n\n```\nATTEMPT 1: Diagnose & Fix\n  → Read error carefully\n  → Identify root cause\n  → Apply targeted fix\n\nATTEMPT 2: Alternative Approach\n  → Same error? Try different method\n  → Different tool? Different library?\n  → NEVER repeat exact same failing action\n\nATTEMPT 3: Broader Rethink\n  → Question assumptions\n  → Search for solutions\n  → Consider updating the plan\n\nAFTER 3 FAILURES: Escalate to User\n  → Explain what you tried\n  → Share the specific error\n  → Ask for guidance\n```\n\n## Read vs Write Decision Matrix\n\n| Situation | Action | Reason |\n|-----------|--------|--------|\n| Just wrote a file | DON'T read | Content still in context |\n| Viewed image/PDF | Write findings NOW | Multimodal → text before lost |\n| Browser returned data | Write to file | Screenshots don't persist |\n| Starting new phase | Read plan/findings | Re-orient if context stale |\n| Error occurred | Read relevant file | Need current state to fix |\n| Resuming after gap | Read all planning files | Recover state |\n\n## The 5-Question Reboot Test\n\nIf you can answer these, your context management is solid:\n\n| Question | Answer Source |\n|----------|---------------|\n| Where am I? | Current phase in task_plan.md |\n| Where am I going? | Remaining phases |\n| What's the goal? | Goal statement in plan |\n| What have I learned? | findings.md |\n| What have I done? | progress.md |\n\n## When to Use This Pattern\n\n**Use for:**\n- Multi-step tasks (3+ steps)\n- Research tasks\n- Building/creating projects\n- Tasks spanning many tool calls\n- Anything requiring organization\n\n**Skip for:**\n- Simple questions\n- Single-file edits\n- Quick lookups\n\n## Templates\n\nCopy these templates to start:\n\n- [templates/task_plan.md](templates/task_plan.md) — Phase tracking\n- [templates/findings.md](templates/findings.md) — Research storage\n- [templates/progress.md](templates/progress.md) — Session logging\n\n## Scripts\n\nHelper scripts for automation:\n\n- `scripts/init-session.sh` — Initialize all planning files\n- `scripts/check-complete.sh` — Verify all phases complete\n\n## Advanced Topics\n\n- **Manus Principles:** See [reference.md](reference.md)\n- **Real Examples:** See [examples.md](examples.md)\n\n## Anti-Patterns\n\n| Don't | Do Instead |\n|-------|------------|\n| Use TodoWrite for persistence | Create task_plan.md file |\n| State goals once and forget | Re-read plan before decisions |\n| Hide errors and retry silently | Log errors to plan file |\n| Stuff everything in context | Store large content in files |\n| Start executing immediately | Create plan file FIRST |\n| Repeat failed actions | Track attempts, mutate approach |\n| Create files in skill directory | Create files in your project |\n\n\nARGUMENTS: Organize 100+ academic/professional documents (PDF, PPTX, DOCX) into 5 folders: LLM, trapped_ion_and_qc, black_hole, DNA, music_history. The black hole\nentropy law inspired the formulation of the holographic principle by ’t Hooft and\nSusskind, which is famously realized in the gauge/gravity correspondence by Maldacena, Gubser–Klebanov–Polaykov and Witten within string theory. In this book chapter we review these developments over five decades, starting in the 1960ies.\n\nIntroduction and Prehistory\nIntroductory remarks. The history of black hole thermodynamics is intertwined\nwith the history of quantum gravity. In the absence of experimental data capable of\nprobing Planck scale physics the best we can do is to subject putative theories of\nquantum gravity to stringent consistency checks. We extend a recently\ndeveloped test charge theory [S. Buyukdagli et al., Phys. Rev. E 94, 042502 (2016)] to the case of\na stiff polymer interacting with a DNA molecule in an electrolyte mixture. The theory accounts for\none-loop level electrostatic correlation effects such as the ionic cloud deformation around the strongly\ncharged DNA molecule as well as image-charge forces induced by the low DNA permittivity. Our\nmodel can reproduce and explain various characteristics of the experimental phase diagrams for\npolymer solutions. First, the addition of polyvalent cations to the electrolyte solution results in\nthe attraction of the negatively charged polymer by the DNA molecule. The glue of the like-charge\nattraction is the enhanced shielding of the polymer charges by the dense counterion layer at the\nDNA surface. Secondly, through the shielding of the DNA-induced electrostatic potential, mono- and\npolyvalent cations of large concentration both suppress the like-charge attraction. Within the same\nformalism, we also predict a new opposite-charge repulsion effect between the DNA molecule and a\npositively charged polymer. In the presence of polyvalent anions such as sulfate or phosphate, their\nrepulsion by the DNA charges leads to the charge screening deficiency of the region around the DNA\nmolecule. This translates into a repulsive force that results in the decomplexation of the polymer\nfrom DNA. This opposite-charge repulsion phenomenon can be verified by current experiments and\nthe underlying mechanism can be beneficial to gene therapeutic applications where the control over\npolymer-DNA interactions is the key factor.\nPACS numbers: 05.20.Jj,82.45.Gj,82.35.Rs\n\nI.\n\n\n=== 1708.07404v3.pdf ===\nMass of Schwarzschild Black Holes Is Indeed Zero And Black Hole Candidates Are\nQuasi Black Holes\nAbhas Mitra∗\n\narXiv:1708.07404v3 [physics.gen-ph] 10 May 2018\n\nHomi Bhabha National Institute, Mumbai-400094, India\n(Dated: May 11, 2018)\nA Schwarzschild Black Hole (BH) is the gravitational field due to a neutral point mass, and it\nturns out that the gravitational mass of a neutral point mass: M = 0 (Arnowitt, Deser, Misner, PRL\n4, 375, 1960). The same result is also suggested by Janis, Newman, and Winicour (PRL 20, 878,\n1968). In 1969, Bel gave an explicit proof that for a Schwarzschild BH, M = 0 (Bel, JMP 10, 1051,\n1969). The same result follows from the fact the timelike geodesic of a test particle would turn null\nif it would ever arrive at an event horizon (Mitra, FPL, 2000, 2002). Non-occurrrence of trapped\nsurfaces in continued gravitational collapse too demands M = 0 for black hole (Mitra, Pramana,\n73, 615, 2009). Physically, for a point mass at R = 0, one expects Ric ∼ M δ(R = 0) (Narlikar &\nPadmanabhan, Found. Phys., 18, 659, 1988). But the black hole solution is obtained from Ric = 0.\nAgain this is the most direct proof that M = 0 for a Schwarzschild black hole. Implication of\nthis result is that the observed massive black hole candidates are non-singular quasi black holes or\nblack hole mimickers which can possess strong magnetic fields as has been observed. The echoes\nfrom LIGO signals, if true, may be the direct evidence that the pertinent compact objects are BH\nmimickers and not true vacuum BHs.\nINTRODUCTION\n\nStarting from Einstein, most of the founding fathers of general relativity thought that though the vacuum\nSchwarzschild equation is correct, its interpretation in terms of black holes is incorrect. Essentially, they pleaded\nthat while a massive star can contract almost upto its Schwarzschild surface, it cannot contract upto the redline\nR = Rs = 2M (G = c = 1), and its radius R > 2M . The term ‘scale’\n  1524→comes from the Latin word ‘scala’ meaning ‘ladder’. Thus a scale\n  1525→is a ladder or a set of notes ordered according to their frequency or\n  1526→pitch. A scale ordered by increasing pitch is an ascending scale,\n  1527→and a scale ordered by decreasing pitch is a descending scale.\n  1528→Scales, in general, may or may not contain the same pitches while\n  1529→ascending as well as when descending. Moreover, even all of the\n  1530→scale steps may not be equal. Indeed, most of the traditional music scales began with unequal scale steps. The term ‘scale’\ncomes from the Latin word ‘scala’ meaning ‘ladder’. Thus a scale\nis a ladder or a set of notes ordered according to their frequency or\npitch. A scale ordered by increasing pitch is an ascending scale,\nand a scale ordered by decreasing pitch is a descending scale.\nScales, in general, may or may not contain the same pitches while\nascending as well as when descending. Moreover, even all of the\nscale steps may not be equal. Indeed, most of the traditional music scales began with unequal scale steps. Due to the principle\nof octave equivalence, scales are generally considered to span a\nsingle octave, with higher or lower octaves simply repeating the\npattern. A musical scale represents a division of the octave space\ninto a specific number of scale steps, a scale step being the interval between two successive notes of the scale.\n\nSushan Konar works on\nstellar compact objects. Malinowski,1, ∗ D. T. C. Allcock,1, 2 and C. J. Ballance1, 3\n1\nOxford Ionics, Oxford, OX5 1PF, UK\nDepartment of Physics, University of Oregon, Eugene, OR 97403\n3\nDepartment of Physics, University of Oxford, Clarendon Laboratory, Parks Road, Oxford, OX1 3PU, UK\n\narXiv:2305.12773v1 [quant-ph] 22 May 2023\n\n2\n\nOne of the most formidable challenges of scaling up quantum computers is that of control signal\ndelivery. Today’s small-scale quantum computers typically connect each qubit to one or more\nseparate external signal sources. This approach is not scalable due to the I/O limitations of the\nqubit chip, necessitating the integration of control electronics. However, it is no small feat to shrink\ncontrol electronics into a small package that is compatible with qubit chip fabrication and operation\nconstraints without sacrificing performance. This so-called “wiring challenge” is likely to impact\nthe development of more powerful quantum computers even in the near term. In this paper, we\naddress the wiring challenge of trapped-ion quantum computers. We describe a control architecture\ncalled WISE (Wiring using Integrated Switching Electronics), which significantly reduces the I/O\nrequirements of ion trap quantum computing chips without compromising performance. Our method\nrelies on judiciously integrating simple switching electronics into the ion trap chip – in a way that\nis compatible with its fabrication and operation constraints – while complex electronics remain\nexternal. ===\nSimulating the flight gate assignment problem on a trapped ion quantum computer\nYahui Chai,1 Evgeny Epifanovsky,2 Karl Jansen,1, 3 Ananth Kaushik,2 and Stefan Kühn1\n\narXiv:2309.09686v1 [quant-ph] 18 Sep 2023\n\n1\n\nCQTA, Deutsches Elektronen-Synchrotron DESY, Platanenallee 6, 15738 Zeuthen, Germany\n2\nIonQ Inc, 4505 Campus Dr, College Park, Maryland 20740, USA\n3\nComputation-Based Science and Technology Research Center,\nThe Cyprus Institute, 20 Kavafi Street, 2121 Nicosia, Cyprus\n(Dated: September 19, 2023)\n\nWe study the flight gate assignment problem on IonQ’s Aria trapped ion quantum computer using the variational quantum eigensolver. Pretraining on vast web-scale data has laid the foundation for these models, yet the research community is now\nincreasingly shifting focus toward post-training techniques to achieve further breakthroughs. While pretraining provides a broad\nlinguistic foundation, post-training methods enable LLMs to refine their knowledge, improve reasoning, enhance factual accuracy, and\nalign more effectively with user intents and ethical considerations. Fine-tuning, reinforcement learning, and test-time scaling have\nemerged as critical strategies for optimizing LLMs performance, ensuring robustness, and improving adaptability across various\nreal-world tasks. This survey provides a systematic exploration of post-training methodologies, analyzing their role in refining LLMs\nbeyond pretraining, addressing key challenges such as catastrophic forgetting, reward hacking, and inference-time trade-offs.
